OPENING
SEO is not dead. The interface changed.
Search was never really about ten blue links. It was about a person with a problem trying to find
someone who could solve it. That has not changed. What changed is the surface the answer arrives on —
and how much of the decision is made before anyone visits a website.
Your buyer now searches across Google, AI Overviews, Google AI Mode, ChatGPT, Claude, Perplexity, Gemini, Reddit, YouTube,
LinkedIn, review sites, marketplaces, directories, and private communities. Often several of these in one afternoon, for one
decision.
The fundamentals still hold. Relevance, authority, trust, technical accessibility, and content that actually answers the question are
as load-bearing as they have ever been. What sits on top of them is new: discoverability across surfaces you do not control,
extractability so a system can quote you safely, entity clarity so a model knows who you are, structured facts that stay consistent
everywhere you appear, and citation worthiness — the thing that decides whether you are named or merely summarized.
Modern SEO optimizes for two readers at once: a person deciding whether to trust you, and a machine deciding whether to repeat
you.
WHERE BUYERS ACTUALLY SEARCH NOW
CLASSIC SEARCH
–
Google web results
–
Bing
–
Image and video search
–
News and Discover
AI ANSWER SURFACES
–

–

–

–

–

–

COMMUNITY & SOCIAL
–
Reddit
–
YouTube
–
LinkedIn
–
Forums and Slack
–
Review sites
MARKETPLACES / DIRECTORIES
–
App and software directories
–
Industry associations
–
Comparison sites
–
Maps and local listings
One brand, four surface families, one underlying requirement: be findable, understandable, and credible on each.
WHAT THIS PLAYBOOK WILL NOT PROMISE
No action described here guarantees a ranking, a click, a citation, an AI recommendation, traffic, or revenue. Search systems and AI
answer engines change their behaviour without notice and do not publish their weighting. What this playbook gives you is a system that
improves the probability of being found, understood, trusted, cited, and recommended — and, just as usefully, a way to tell whether it is
working.

HOW TO BUILD THIS IN NOTION
Each of the ten operating areas becomes a page. Each database described in Section 11 becomes a Notion database with the properties
listed there. The SOPs in Section 14 become a single SOP database with one entry per procedure, and the prompt library in Section 13
becomes a prompt database you filter by task. The dashboard in Section 11 is the home page that links them together. Nothing here
requires a paid tier or a template purchase.

SECTION 01 · WHAT THIS IS
The complete blueprint
This is not a guide to building an SEO workspace. It is the workspace itself, written out — every page,
database, property, relation, view, formula, template, SOP, prompt, and example required to reproduce
it.
This playbook contains the complete architecture and content of The AI SEO Brain. Each folder below represents a page or
operating area in the Notion workspace. Each database includes its properties, relationships, views, formulas, templates,
workflows, and example records.
WHAT YOU ARE RECEIVING, STATED HONESTLY
This is a PDF blueprint and operating manual — a complete, implementation-ready specification you rebuild in your own Notion
workspace. It is not a live Notion template and no duplication link is included. Nothing here connects to Google Search Console, your
crawler, or any AI engine automatically; every integration described is something you would build or operate manually. Rebuild time is
roughly six to ten hours for the full system, or about ninety minutes for the minimum viable version described in Section 18.
The asset inventory
Everything in this document, counted honestly. No asset appears twice under a different name.

WHAT IS DELIBERATELY ABSENT
No guaranteed rankings, traffic, citations, or AI recommendations. No invented statistics, benchmarks, or research findings. No claim that

tactics. Every company, metric, and record shown as an example is fictional and labelled as such.

HOW THIS MAPS TO THE ORIGINAL TEN AREAS
Operating areas 01 through 10 are the same ten from the original framework, renamed only where the expanded scope required it —
Market & Audience Intelligence, Keyword Intelligence, Topic & Content System, On-Page & Answer Readiness, and LLM & AI Visibility.
Areas 00 and 11 through 16 are the operating layer that makes the other ten repeatable: the command center, measurement, the three
libraries, experiments, and the decision log.

SECTION 04 · STANDARD
The Notion database standard
Every one of the thirty-two databases in this system follows the same specification. Once you can read
one, you can build all of them.
SECTION
WHAT IT SPECIFIES
WHY IT IS THERE
Header
Purpose, owner, who uses it, inputs, outputs
So nobody has to guess what the database is for or who maintains it
Properties
Name, Notion type, allowed values, required flag, default,
definition, example
So the database can be built exactly, without interpretation
Relations
Destination database, cardinality, purpose
Relations are where most Notion builds go wrong — they are
specified explicitly
Rollups
Source relation, property, calculation, why it matters
So the numbers on a card come from data rather than from manual
entry
Formulas
The exact expression where Notion supports it, or the
method where it does not
So scores calculate themselves and stay comparable
Views
Name, type, filters, sort, grouping, intended user, decision
supported
A view exists to support one decision — if it does not, it is clutter
Template
A database template with pre-filled sections and a
completion checklist
So new records start complete rather than empty
Workflow
Status values, allowed transitions, blocking conditions,
definition of done
So a record cannot advance past a stage it has not satisfied
Cadence, archive, gate
Review frequency, archive rule, quality gate
So the database stays useful instead of becoming a graveyard
Example records
Three fictional records for the most important databases
So you can see what a completed record actually looks like
Property conventions used throughout
Applied consistently across all thirty-two databases.
CONVENTION
RULE
REASON
Required flag
R = required before the record leaves Draft status
Prevents half-complete records entering a workflow
Unknown values
Left empty, never estimated or filled with a placeholder
number
An estimated value becomes an apparent fact within a month
Dates
Every measurement carries an as-of date property
A number without a date cannot be compared to anything
Sources
Every factual property has a paired Source property
Provenance is what makes a record reviewable later
Owner
Person type, never text — one named human per record
Text owners drift; a Person property can be filtered and notified
Scores
Number properties feeding a Formula, never a manually
entered total
Manual totals stop matching their components within weeks
Status
Select with a fixed set, never multi-select
A record in two states cannot be reported on
Archive
A Checkbox plus an Archived date, never deletion
Deleted records take their reasoning with them
BUILD THE PROPERTIES BEFORE THE RELATIONS
Notion will let you create a relation to a database that does not exist yet, and the resulting mess is the single most common reason people
abandon a workspace build halfway through. Create all thirty-two databases with their properties first, then add relations in the order
given in Section 18, then rollups, then formulas. That order is not a preference — it is a dependency chain.

THE SEO MATURITY ASSESSMENT
LEVEL 1
Reactive
SEO happens when someone remembers or a problem appears
LEVEL 2
Tactical
Keywords and content exist, but nothing connects to a goal
LEVEL 3
Structured
A documented strategy, a backlog, and a review cadence
LEVEL 4
Systematic
One workspace, defined SOPs, owners, and quality gates
LEVEL 5
Compounding
Every cycle updates the system that produced it
Most teams sit at Level 2 and believe they are at Level 3. The difference is whether the strategy exists as a document anyone could follow.
The SEO maturity assessment
Score each dimension 1 to 5 against the levels above. Your maturity is your lowest score, not your average.
DIMENSION
THE QUESTION TO ASK HONESTLY
Strategy
Could a new hire read one document and know what we are trying to win?
Demand understanding
Do we know what buyers ask, in their words, with evidence?
Keyword system
Is there one list everyone works from, with a scoring rule?
Content system
Is there a brief, a standard, and a refresh cadence?
Technical health
Do we know our issues, ranked, with owners and tickets?
Internal linking
Do we know which money pages have no internal links?
Authority
Do we know who cites us and why, and where the gaps are?
AI visibility
Do we know what AI systems say about us today, tracked over time?
Measurement
Can we connect an SEO action to a business metric?
Governance
Is there an owner, a cadence, and a place decisions are recorded?

If your strategy does not fit here, it is a task list rather than a strategy.

The modern SEO principles checklist
Run this quarterly. Anything unchecked becomes a backlog item with an owner.
A written strategy exists and names what we will not pursue
Every priority page has a stated intent and a stated conversion path
Content is written from customer problems, in customer language
Authors are named and their credentials are verifiable
Claims on the site are sourced or removed
Every important page is reachable in three clicks or fewer
Crawl and index status is checked on a cadence, not on a hunch
Structured data is present, valid, and honest about the page
Key facts about the brand are identical everywhere they appear
Content is extractable — a system could quote one passage safely
Internal links point at commercially important pages
Measurement connects at least one SEO metric to a business
objective
THE ORDER MATTERS MORE THAN THE LIST
Teams routinely work on authority and AI visibility while their own site is partly unindexable and their money pages have no internal links.
Foundations first is not conservatism — it is the only sequence where later work compounds instead of leaking.

SECTION 02 · DEMAND
Market, audience, and search demand
Keyword tools tell you what has already been typed enough times to be measured. This section is about
understanding the problem before you accept a tool’s framing of it.
Almost every weak SEO programme starts the same way: someone opens a keyword tool, exports a list, and begins. The list is real,
but it is a record of the past, phrased in the vocabulary that already ranks. It contains no emerging demand, none of the language
your customers use in private, and nothing about the questions people now ask an assistant instead of a search box.
Demand research comes first. It costs a week and changes everything downstream.

The highest-value input to keyword research
Separating real demand from vanity topics
Signs of real demand
•
Multiple customers described it unprompted
•
It appears in lost-deal reasons or support volume
•
It maps to a decision someone has to make
•
People search it with commercial or comparison intent
•
You have something specific to say that others do not
Signs of a vanity topic
•
High volume, no relationship to what you sell
•
Interesting to your team, not to a buyer
•
Every competitor has covered it identically
•
Traffic would arrive and leave with nothing to do
•
You would be summarizing rather than contributing
Zero-click and AI-answer opportunities

Page 26 / 222

The keyword scoring model
Ten criteria, scored 0–5. Transparent, adjustable, and openly imperfect.
CRITERION
SCORE 0
SCORE 5
WEIGHT
Business relevance
Unrelated to what we sell
Directly precedes a purchase decision
×3
Search demand
No measurable or evidenced demand
Strong, sustained, evidenced demand
×2
Intent match
Intent conflicts with our page type
Intent matches exactly what we offer
×3
Ranking difficulty
Dominated by far stronger domains
Weak, outdated results we can beat
×2
Conversion potential
Traffic with nowhere to go
Clear next step and a real offer
×3
Current authority
No topical presence at all
We already rank nearby and are trusted here
×1
Content gap
Well covered by everyone including us
A real gap we can fill distinctively
×2
AI answer potential
Cannot be summarized safely
Extractable, factual, quotable
×2
Brand fit
Off-positioning or off-audience
Reinforces exactly what we stand for
×1
Strategic value
Isolated, leads nowhere
Anchors a cluster or defends a position
×2
THE PRIORITIZATION FORMULA
PRIORITY SCORE = sum of (criterion score x weight)
Maximum possible = 105
Suggested bands:
  80-105 Build now — cluster anchor or revenue page
  60-79 Build this quarter
  40-59 Build when capacity allows, or fold into an existing page
  20-39 Monitor only
  0-19 Decline and record the reason
ADJUST THE WEIGHTS. They are a starting point reflecting a typical B2B
situation, not a scientific model. If conversion matters more than volume in
your business, raise it. If you are pre-authority, raise ranking difficulty.
Record every weight change with a date so scores stay comparable.
BE HONEST ABOUT WHAT A SCORE IS
This model makes decisions consistent and reviewable. It does not make them correct. Two competent SEOs will score the same keyword
differently, and the weights are judgement expressed as numbers. Its value is that it forces the judgement to be written down, so it can be
argued with and improved — not that it produces objective truth.

Keyword-to-page mapping, and the cannibalization audit
One primary keyword, one owning page. Everything else is a supporting variation.
#
AUDIT STEP
WHAT YOU ARE LOOKING FOR
1
Export every keyword with its ranking URL over the
last 90 days
Queries where the ranking URL changes back and forth
2
Group by keyword and count distinct ranking URLs
Two or more of your pages competing for one query
3
For each conflict, identify the intended owner
Which page should win, given intent and conversion path
4
Decide: consolidate, differentiate, de-optimize, or
redirect
A decision per conflict, recorded with a reason
5
Check internal links point at the intended owner
Links pulling authority towards the page you decided to demote
6
Re-check after 60 days
Whether the intended page now owns the query consistently

The AI SEO Brain  ·  Offered by Klaym AI — klaym.ai
Page 28 / 222

THE MOST COMMON KEYWORD MISTAKE
Mapping many similar keywords to many similar pages, in the belief that more pages means more coverage. It produces a set of pages
that each rank weakly, compete with each other, split internal link equity, and are individually too thin to be cited by anything. One strong
page beats four adequate ones on every surface discussed in this playbook.

SECTION 04 · CONTENT
Content strategy
Publishing is not a strategy. This section is about deciding what to build, in what order, how deep, and —
the decision most teams never make — what to stop maintaining.
The hub-and-spoke system
One pillar, six to twelve supporting pieces, linked in both directions. This is what produces topical authority rather than a pile of posts.
LAYER
WHAT IT IS
WHAT IT DOES
HOW MANY
Pillar page
The comprehensive page on the core
topic
Ranks for the head term, holds the cluster
together
One per cluster
Supporting content
Depth on one sub-question each
Ranks for long-tail, links up to the pillar
6–12 per cluster
Commercial page
The product, category, or pricing page
The destination the cluster points at
1–2 per cluster
Comparison content
Honest vs and alternative pages
Captures vendor-aware intent and is
frequently cited
2–5 per cluster
Reference assets
Glossary, templates, tools, data
Earns links, gets cited, defends the cluster
As the topic warrants
The create / update / merge / redirect / remove decision
Run this on every existing page once a quarter. Most teams have more to fix than to write.
IF THE PAGE...
AND...
THEN
WHY
Ranks and converts
Nothing has changed in the
topic
Leave it alone
Do not touch what is working
Ranks but does not convert
Intent is commercial
Fix the conversion path
The traffic is right, the destination is wrong
Ranked and is now sliding
Competitors have gone deeper
Update against what overtook it
Refreshing beats writing new almost every
time

Position 4–10
Rewrite title and meta
This is a copy problem, not a ranking problem

Both rank weakly for one query
Merge into the stronger URL
One strong page beats two adequate ones
Has no traffic and no links
Live for over a year
Remove and redirect
Thin pages dilute crawl and topical clarity
Has links but no traffic
The topic is no longer relevant
Redirect to the closest live page
Preserve the equity, drop the page
Is factually out of date
It is still cited or linked
Update urgently
Outdated cited content is a credibility risk on
AI surfaces

THE REFRESH-FIRST RULE
Before commissioning anything new, check the decay monitor. A page that once ranked and is now sliding will almost always recover
faster, and for less effort, than a new page will climb. Most content teams have this backwards, because writing is more satisfying than
editing.

SECTION 05 · ON-PAGE
On-page SEO
Making one page as easy as possible to understand — for a person skimming it, a crawler parsing it, and
a model deciding whether it can be quoted without risk.
On-page work has quietly changed job. It used to be about signalling relevance to a ranking algorithm. It is now equally about being
extractable: can a system lift one passage from this page, present it as an answer, and attribute it to you without introducing an
error?
A page that is genuinely clear performs well at both jobs. A page written to game one usually fails the other.
The on-page checklist
Run before publication. Nothing here is optional on a commercially important page.
ELEMENT
THE STANDARD
THE COMMON FAILURE
Title
Primary keyword, real benefit, roughly 60 characters
Keyword stuffing, or a title that describes nothing
Meta description
Accurate summary that earns the click
Auto-generated, duplicated, or unrelated to the page
URL
Short, readable, stable, no dates or parameters
Deep nesting and IDs that make the URL meaningless
H1
One per page, matching the search intent
Multiple H1s, or an H1 that differs from the promise
Heading structure
A hierarchy a person could outline from
Headings chosen for styling rather than structure
Introduction
Answers the question in the first two sentences
Three paragraphs of context before the answer
Intent match
The page type matches what the query wants
A blog post targeting a query that wants a product page
Semantic coverage
Related concepts covered because they belong
Keyword variations sprinkled without substance
Internal links
Contextual, descriptive anchors, pointing at money
pages
"Click here", or no links to anything commercial
External references
Sources cited where a claim needs support
Unsupported statistics with no origin
Images and alt text
Compressed, described accurately
Alt text stuffed with keywords or left empty
Tables and lists
Used where structure genuinely aids
comprehension
Walls of prose that should have been a table
Definitions
Key terms defined once, clearly, in one place
Terms used before they are explained
FAQs
Real questions from the Question Bank, answered
directly
Invented questions padded for schema markup
CTA
One clear next step matched to the reader’s stage
Four competing CTAs, or none at all
ON-PAGE SEO
The AI SEO Brain  ·  Offered by Klaym AI — klaym.ai
Page 33 / 222

The extractability checklist
This is the part most on-page guidance still omits. It decides whether you are cited or merely summarized.
The direct answer appears within the first 100 words, before any
preamble
Each key answer is self-contained — it makes sense lifted out of
the page
No answer depends on a previous paragraph to be understood
Facts are stated plainly, not implied through narrative
Numbers carry their unit, their period, and their source
Definitions are single sentences that could stand alone
Lists and steps are genuinely parallel and complete
Tables have real headers rather than styled first rows
Comparisons state the criterion before the verdict
Nothing important lives only inside an image or a video
The page renders fully without JavaScript execution
Structured data matches what a human sees on the page
The author, the publication date, and the last update are visible
Claims that cannot be supported have been removed rather than
softened
The AI citation readiness checklist
A narrower question: could a system quote this page and be confident it is correct?
REQUIREMENT
WHY IT MATTERS
HOW TO CHECK
One unambiguous claim per
passage
Ambiguity makes a passage unsafe to
quote
Read one paragraph alone — is it still true and clear?
Facts consistent across your own
site
Self-contradiction undermines
everything else
Search your domain for the same fact stated differently
Facts consistent with third
parties
Corroboration carries weight
Check your directory listings, profiles, and about page
Dated and attributed
Freshness and authorship affect trust
Visible publication date, update date, and named author
Sources for anything
non-obvious
An unsourced claim is risky to repeat
Every statistic links to its origin
No overclaiming
Superlatives make a page less quotable,
not more
Remove "best", "leading", "#1" unless externally verified

SECTION 06 · TECHNICAL
Technical SEO
Everything above this section assumes machines can reach, render, and read your pages. This layer
decides whether that assumption holds — and it is where fixes are cheapest per unit of effect.
The prioritization model
Technical backlogs fail because everything looks urgent. Score every issue the same way.
ISSUE PRIORITIZATION FORMULA
PRIORITY = (Impact x Affected Pages x Business Importance x Confidence) / Effort
Impact 1-5 How much does fixing this change discoverability?
Affected pages 1-5 1 = a handful, 5 = sitewide
Business importance 1-5 Are the affected pages commercially important?
Confidence 1-5 How sure are we this is actually the cause?
Effort 1-5 1 = minutes, 5 = a project
Confidence is the term teams omit, and it is the one that prevents a quarter
of engineering time being spent on a hypothesis. If confidence is below 3,
the next action is a diagnostic, not a fix.
The severity matrix
SEVERITY
EXAMPLES
RESPONSE
Critical — site level
Robots.txt blocking, sitewide noindex, site down,
certificate failure
Immediate incident response, fixed within hours
High — section level
A template unindexable, broken canonicals, key content
rendered only by JS
Next sprint, with a named engineering owner
Medium — page level
Slow templates, redirect chains, orphan money pages,
missing structured data
Scheduled into the backlog by priority score
Low — cosmetic
Minor markup warnings, image sizing, non-critical alt text
Batched, or fixed alongside other work
The technical audit checklist
Twenty-six checks across four groups. Run quarterly, and after any release that touches templates.
Crawl and index
•
Robots.txt permits what should be crawled
•
XML sitemaps current, containing only indexable URLs
•
Index coverage matches the page count you intend
•
Canonicals self-referencing or deliberate
•
No redirect chains longer than one hop
•
Status codes correct — no soft 404s
•
Orphan pages identified, then linked or removed
•
Crawl budget not consumed by filters and pagination
Rendering and performance
•
Key content renders without JavaScript
•
Core Web Vitals measured on field data, not lab only
•
Mobile usability verified on real devices
•
Images compressed and correctly sized
•
Server response time stable under load
•
No render-blocking resources on critical templates
•
HTTPS enforced everywhere, no mixed content
•
Pagination handled consistently
Structure and duplication
•
Site architecture reachable in three clicks
•
Faceted navigation controlled, not indexable by default
•
Duplicate content consolidated or canonicalized
•
Parameter handling documented
•
Breadcrumbs present and marked up
International, markup, and monitoring
•
Hreflang complete, reciprocal, and valid where used
•
Structured data present, valid, matching visible content
•
Broken internal and external links identified
•
Log files reviewed for where crawlers actually spend time
•
Security headers and certificate validity checked
TECHNICAL SEO
The AI SEO Brain  ·  Offered by Klaym AI — klaym.ai

Migration and relaunch checklist
Most catastrophic traffic losses are migrations that skipped one of these.
Full crawl of the current site archived before anything changes
Complete URL inventory with current traffic, rankings, and
backlinks
Redirect map covering every URL, one hop, tested on staging
Canonical, hreflang, and structured data ported and validated
Robots.txt and sitemaps prepared for the new structure
Analytics and Search Console configured before launch, not after
Internal links updated to final URLs, not pointing through redirects
Staging environment blocked from indexing, and verified as
blocked
Launch window chosen to avoid the highest-traffic period
Post-launch crawl within 24 hours, and again at 7 and 30 days
Rollback plan written, tested, and owned by a named person
Rankings and index coverage baselined for comparison

SECTION 07 · ARCHITECTURE
Internal linking and information architecture
The cheapest and most under-used lever in SEO. You already own every page involved, no outreach is
required, and the effect is usually visible within weeks.
Internal linking does three jobs at once. It distributes authority towards the pages that matter commercially. It tells search systems
and models how your topics relate. And it moves a reader from a question towards a decision. Most sites do the first two by
accident and the third not at all.
The internal linking audit
Six checks. The first alone usually finds work worth a quarter of content production.
#
CHECK
WHAT YOU ARE LOOKING FOR
TYPICAL FINDING
1
Money pages with few internal
links
Commercially important pages nothing
points at
The pricing page has fewer links than a blog post
2
Orphan pages
Pages reachable only from the sitemap
Older content nobody linked to after publication
3
Click depth
Important pages more than three clicks from
home
A whole category buried under pagination
4
Anchor text distribution
Descriptive anchors versus generic ones
"Learn more" pointing at your priority page
5
Blog-to-product links
Whether informational content leads
anywhere
Hundreds of posts with no commercial link
6
Link decay
Links lost in redesigns and template changes
A navigation change that quietly removed a hub
The link priority score
Which internal link opportunity to build first.
LINK PRIORITY SCORE
LINK PRIORITY = Target page value x Topical relevance x Source page strength
Target page value 1-5 How commercially important is the destination?
Topical relevance 1-5 Would a reader genuinely want this link here?
Source page strength 1-5 Does the linking page have traffic and links itself?
Build in descending order. Stop when relevance drops below 3 — an irrelevant
internal link helps nobody and dilutes the ones that matter.
RULE: never add a link a reader would not want to click. Internal linking
done for the algorithm alone is visible to readers and to reviewers, and it
degrades the page it sits on.
Anchor text guidelines
SITUATION
DO
DO NOT
Linking to a commercial page
Use a descriptive phrase matching the page topic
Use "click here", "this page", or a bare URL
Linking to a pillar
Use the topic name as it appears in the H1
Use exact-match keyword text on every instance
Multiple links to one page
Vary the anchor naturally across pages
Repeat identical exact-match anchors sitewide
Linking within a sentence
Link the phrase describing the destination
Link a whole sentence, or a single word like "here"
Navigation and footers
Keep them stable and meaningful
Stuff the footer with keyword anchors to everything

SECTION 08 · AUTHORITY
Link building, digital PR, and authority
Links are one expression of authority, not the whole of it. This section covers the six kinds of authority
that matter now, and how to build each without doing anything you would have to hide.
Six kinds of authority, and why the distinction matters
TYPE
WHAT IT IS
HOW IT IS BUILT
WHERE IT SHOWS UP
Link acquisition
Other sites linking to yours
Assets worth referencing, plus outreach
Classic ranking signals

People search for you by name
Consistent presence, product quality, and
reputation
Branded search volume, direct traffic

Systems know who you are and what
you do

Knowledge panels, AI answers

Depth and coverage on one subject

You are quoted as a source

passages

Everywhere — it gates all of the above

The digital PR campaign template
TEMPLATE — DIGITAL PR CAMPAIGN
# Digital PR Campaign — [name]
THE ASSET: [what exists that is worth referencing]
THE STORY: [the one sentence a journalist would write]
WHY NOW: [the timing hook — a season, a change, a live debate]
WHY US: [what makes us the credible source for this specifically]
THE DATA: [what we can share, from where, with what permission]
WHAT WE CANNOT CLAIM: [limits of the data — state these before outreach]
METHODOLOGY: [published alongside — this is what makes it citable]
TARGET PUBLICATIONS: [type, not just names — trade, national, niche, newsletter]
TARGET JOURNALISTS: [who covers this beat, with a link to recent work]
WHAT THEY GET: [the angle for their audience, not for ours]
ASSETS PREPARED: [data file, charts, quotes, spokesperson bio, images]
SPOKESPERSON: [named, with the credential that matters here]
OUTREACH SEQUENCE: [first contact, follow-up, close — max three touches]
NOT DOING: [no paid placements, no link exchanges, no undisclosed sponsorship]
SUCCESS SIGNALS: [coverage, citations, mentions, referral traffic]
OWNER: [name] WINDOW: [dates]
Outreach template library
Four situations, four openings. Each under 120 words in practice.
SITUATION
THE OPENING MOVE
THE ASK
Journalist, live story
Reference the specific piece they just published
Offer a named source and a data point, no link request
Resource page
Name the page and what your asset adds that is missing
Suggest the addition, and accept a no
Unlinked mention
Thank them for the mention, note the correct URL
Ask whether they would be willing to link it
Original research pitch
Lead with the finding, not with your company
Offer the data, the methodology, and an interview
WHAT THIS PLAYBOOK WILL NOT HELP YOU DO
Buying links, private blog networks, link exchanges, undisclosed sponsored placements, automated comment or forum links, scraped
guest-post networks, and anything that requires hiding the arrangement. These violate search engine guidelines, carry real penalty risk,
and — increasingly relevant — do nothing for entity or citation authority, which is where the growth now is. If a tactic only works while
nobody knows about it, it is not in this playbook.

The toxic-link review checklist
Most sites need this far less often than tools suggest. Review before you disavow anything.
The link genuinely came from a manipulative source, not just a low-metric one
It is part of a pattern, not an isolated scrape or aggregator
You did not build it yourself, or it was built by a previous agency
Removal was attempted first, and documented
There is a plausible connection to an actual ranking problem
The decision and its reasoning are recorded before any disavow file is filed

SECTION 09 · LOCAL
Local SEO
For anyone who serves a place: a service business, a multi-location brand, or a company whose buyers
care where you are. Local visibility runs on different signals and rewards consistency above almost
everything else.
The local SEO checklist
Five groups. NAP consistency is the one that quietly breaks everything else.
Profile and listings
•
Google Business Profile claimed and fully completed
•
Primary category correct, secondary categories deliberate
•
Hours accurate, including holidays
•
Services and products listed with descriptions
•
Photos current and genuinely representative
•
NAP identical across every listing, character for character
•
Core citations claimed and consistent
•
Duplicate listings identified and merged
Site and content
•
One landing page per location, with unique content
•
Service-area pages that describe real service, not just a place name
•
Location schema present and matching the profile
•
Embedded map and directions on each location page
•
Local-intent keywords covered in real language
•
Local content that would interest a local reader
•
Internal links from the site to each location page
•
Mobile experience verified — most local search is mobile
The local landing page template
TEMPLATE — LOCAL LANDING PAGE
# [Service] in [Location]
H1: [Service] in [Location]
INTRO: What we do here, for whom, and what makes this location specific.
       Two sentences. No stuffed place names.
WHAT WE OFFER HERE: [services actually available at this location]
WHO WE SERVE HERE: [the local customer, described specifically]
LOCAL CONTEXT: [something true about operating in this place — regulations,
                conditions, timing, the local situation]
TEAM: [who works here, named where possible]
ADDRESS AND HOURS: [exactly matching the Business Profile, character for character]
MAP AND DIRECTIONS: [embedded, plus written directions from a landmark]
CONTACT: [local number where one exists]
PROOF: [reviews or work from this location — never invented, never borrowed
        from another location]
FAQS: [real questions this location actually receives]
CTA: [the one action — call, book, visit, request]
SCHEMA: LocalBusiness with address, geo, hours, and telephone matching the
        profile exactly.
Review response prompts and rules
REVIEW TYPE
RESPOND BY
NEVER
Positive and specific
Thanking them and referencing what they
mentioned

Positive but generic
A short, warm, human reply
Adding keywords or a sales pitch
Negative and fair
Acknowledging it, stating what you are doing,
moving offline
Arguing, or disputing the facts in public
Negative and inaccurate
Correcting one fact politely, offering to resolve
directly
A long public rebuttal

Suspected fake

ON REVIEW GENERATION
Ask customers for reviews. Do not offer anything in exchange, do not filter who gets asked based on how happy they seem, do not write
reviews for anyone, and do not use services that supply them. Beyond the platform-policy risk, incentivized and gated reviews are
increasingly detectable and they poison the exact signal you were trying to build.

MULTI-LOCATION: THE ONE RULE THAT SCALES
Maintain one canonical NAP record per location and generate every listing and page from it. The moment addresses are edited

visibility for businesses that are otherwise doing everything right.

SECTION 10 · AI SEO
LLM and AI SEO
The newest surface, and the one with the least reliable folklore around it. What follows is what is
actually observable, what you can legitimately influence, and how to measure whether any of it is
working.
AI answer engines do not rank pages the way a search engine does. They assemble an answer, and in doing so they decide which
sources to draw on and which to name. That decision runs on things you can influence: whether you can be found at all, whether
your facts are unambiguous, whether other credible sources say the same thing, whether your content can be extracted safely, and
whether you are an obvious answer to a specific question.
Nobody outside these companies knows the weighting, and it changes. What follows is a system for improving the inputs and
measuring the outputs — not a formula for being recommended.
THE AI VISIBILITY PROGRESSION
01
FINDABLE
Crawlable, indexable, discoverable on every surface your buyer uses
02
UNDERSTANDABLE
Clear entities, structured facts, unambiguous language and markup
03
CREDIBLE
Corroborated by sources other than you, consistently, over time
04
CITABLE
Extractable in self-contained passages a system can quote safely
05
RECOMMENDABLE
The obvious answer for a specific query, from a source with standing
Each stage depends on the one before it. Nothing here guarantees a recommendation — it changes the probability that one is possible.
What each stage actually requires
STAGE
WHAT INCREASES THE PROBABILITY
HOW YOU CHECK IT
Findable
Indexable pages, presence on third-party sources these systems
draw on, coverage of the question in public places
Ask the assistant a question you should obviously appear for
Understandable
Consistent entity facts, unambiguous naming, structured data,
clear category language, an about page that states plainly what
you do
Ask an assistant to describe your company and check every fact
Credible
Corroboration from sources other than you, named expertise,
consistent facts across the web, real reviews and independent
coverage
Ask what the assistant knows about your reputation and
sources
Citable
Self-contained passages, dated and attributed content, sourced
claims, no overclaiming, facts stated plainly rather than implied
Check whether answers quote you or paraphrase without
attribution
Recommendable
Being a genuinely good answer to a specific question, with
standing in that topic and third-party validation to support it
Track recommendation prompts over time and record who is
named
LLM & AI SEO
The AI SEO Brain  ·  Offered by Klaym AI — klaym.ai
Page 45 / 222

Building the prompt universe
You cannot track AI visibility without deciding what to track. This is the foundational step and most teams skip it.
#
STEP
OUTPUT
1
List the decisions your buyer makes where you should be a
candidate
A decision list, not a keyword list
2
For each decision, write how someone would ask an assistant
about it
Conversational prompts in natural phrasing
3
Add category prompts — "best X for Y", "X alternatives", "how
to choose X"
The prompts where recommendations actually happen
4
Add brand prompts — "what is [brand]", "is [brand] good for
[use case]"
Prompts that test understanding and sentiment
5
Add competitor prompts — "[competitor] vs", "alternatives to
[competitor]"
Where you should appear but may not
6
Add problem prompts in customer language, from the
Question Bank
Early-stage prompts where a first impression forms
7
Cap the set at what you will genuinely re-run on a cadence
Usually 30–60 prompts. Tracking 300 once is worth less than 40 monthly
The AI visibility audit
Run the prompt universe across the assistants your buyers use. Record the same fields every time.
WHAT TO RECORD
WHY IT MATTERS
The exact prompt used
Small wording changes produce different answers — comparability requires consistency
Which assistant and when
Behaviour differs between systems and changes over time
Whether you were mentioned
The base visibility question
Whether you were cited with a link
Mention and citation are different outcomes
Which page was cited
Tells you what kind of content actually gets used
Which competitors were named
Your share of the answer, and who owns the rest
What was said about you
Accuracy matters as much as presence
Any factual error
The highest-priority finding in the whole audit
Which sources the answer drew on
Shows where to build presence next
The brand fact consistency audit
Systems weigh corroboration. If your own facts disagree with each other, none of them are safe to repeat.

# Brand Fact Sheet — the single source of truth
LEGAL NAME: [exact] TRADING NAME: [exact]

  [ ] Homepage and about page [ ] Structured data on the site
  [ ] LinkedIn company page [ ] Crunchbase or equivalent
  [ ] Business Profile listings [ ] Directory and marketplace profiles
  [ ] Press releases and coverage [ ] Founder and team social profiles
  [ ] Review platform profiles [ ] Partner and integration pages
RULE: one canonical wording per fact. Where a source is wrong, correct it at
the source rather than adding a third version.

The citation-gap and source-authority analysis
When an assistant answers a question in your category, where does it get the answer?
#
STEP
WHAT IT TELLS YOU
1
Run your category prompts and record every source cited
The set of places these systems actually draw on
2
Group sources by type — publisher, directory, community,
vendor, review site
Where the category’s credibility currently lives
3
Check which of those sources mention you, and how
Your presence in the pool the answer is built from
4
Identify sources that cover competitors but not you
The citation gap — usually the most actionable finding
5
Assess which gaps are legitimately closable
Editorial coverage, directory listings, community presence, review platforms
6
Rank by effort and by how often the source appears

The AI visibility scorecard
Five measures. Track the trend rather than the absolute number — the absolute number is not comparable to anyone else’s.
MEASURE
HOW TO CALCULATE IT
WHAT TO WATCH
Mention rate
Prompts where you appear / total prompts run
Trend across months, at fixed prompt wording
Citation rate
Prompts where you are cited with a source / total
prompts
The gap between mention and citation — that gap is extractability
Share of answer
Your mentions / total brand mentions across the set
Who owns the category answer, and whether that is shifting
Accuracy rate
Answers about you with zero factual errors / answers
about you
Anything below full accuracy is an entity consistency problem
Recommendation presence
Recommendation prompts where you are among
those named
The hardest measure to move, and the one that matters
commercially

Page 48 / 222

THE HONEST STATEMENT ABOUT ALL OF THIS
No action described in this section guarantees that an AI system will find, cite, or recommend your brand. These systems are opaque, they
change without notice, and two runs of the same prompt on the same day can differ. What you can do is remove the reasons a system
would not use you: be indexable, be factually consistent, be corroborated by others, be extractable, and be genuinely good on a specific
question. Then measure, on a fixed cadence, and let the trend tell you whether it is working.
The entity consistency checklist
 One canonical brand fact sheet exists and has an owner
Company description is identical on site, LinkedIn, and every
directory
Product names are spelled and capitalized the same way
everywhere
Organization structured data is present and matches the about
page
Founder and leadership names match across all public profiles
Category description is consistent — you are one thing, not four
Outdated descriptions from old positioning have been corrected at
source
Third-party profiles are claimed rather than abandoned
Where a source states something wrong, a correction has been
requested
The fact sheet is reviewed quarterly and versioned
LLM & AI SEO

SECTION 03 · COMMAND CENTER
The SEO Brain Command Center
One page. Six blocks. Twenty-eight view configurations. This is what the team opens each morning and
what leadership opens each month.
The Command Center holds no data of its own. Every block is a linked view of a database specified in Section 05, filtered to answer
one question. That is the discipline that keeps it useful: if a block does not answer a question somebody actually asks, it is removed
rather than tolerated.

BUILD THE RISK CENTER FIRST
Of the six blocks, the Risk Center is the one that changes behaviour fastest. It surfaces the seven things that quietly cost the most —
indexation incidents, decay, unsupported claims, brand-fact conflicts, inaccurate AI answers, lost citations, and local drift — none of
which anyone goes looking for voluntarily. Build it in week one, even if the rest of the Command Center waits.
THE COMMAND CENTER

How a module becomes a fix
The same eight steps regardless of which module the issue came from.
THE TECHNICAL MODULES
The AI SEO Brain  ·  Offered by Klaym AI — klaym.ai
Page 58 / 222

STEP
WHAT HAPPENS
RECORDED WHERE
1 · Detect
The module check finds something
Technical Issues, status Found
2 · Evidence
Crawl export, log sample, or screenshot attached
Evidence property
3 · Score
Impact, pages, business, confidence, effort entered
Five score properties
4 · Route
Confidence below 3 becomes a diagnostic, not a ticket
Action formula
5 · Ticket
Dev ticket written with testable acceptance criteria
Ticket URL
6 · Deploy
Engineering ships it in a named release
Releases & Verification
7 · Verify
Production check within 48 hours
Verified date
8 · Watch
7-day and 30-day index checks on the release
Release record
THE FAILURE THIS SEQUENCE EXISTS TO PREVENT
An SEO finds an issue, describes it in a message, engineering interprets it differently, ships something adjacent, and nobody checks
afterwards. Six weeks later the issue is still open and both sides believe the other dropped it. Steps 2, 5, and 7 are the ones that prevent it
— evidence, testable criteria, and a production verification with a date.

What to report and what to withhold
The ten AI visibility measures, and what each cannot tell you
Every measure here is comparable only to your own previous months, at identical prompt wording and assistant set.
MEASURE
METHOD
WHAT IT CANNOT TELL YOU
Mention rate
Runs where we are named ÷ total runs
Whether the mention was favourable, or whether anyone saw it
Citation rate
Runs where we are cited with attribution ÷ total
runs
Whether the cited page was the one you wanted used
Answer coverage
Prompts with a mention on any assistant ÷
prompts run
How consistently you appear — one assistant of three still counts
Share of AI voice
Our mentions ÷ all brand mentions in the set
Market share. It measures a set you defined, nothing more
Sentiment distribution
Manual classification of each answer about us
Anything reliable at small n — classification is a judgement
Recommendation rate
Recommendation prompts naming us ÷
recommendation prompts
Why you were or were not recommended
Competitor overlap
Prompts where both we and a competitor
appear
Whether the comparison favoured either party
Source concentration
Distinct sources cited ÷ total citations observed
Whether those sources are reachable for you
Factual accuracy
Error-free answers about us ÷ answers about us
Errors on prompts you did not include in the set
Trend change
This month minus last month, per measure
Causation. A change may follow your work or something the system
changed
THE HONEST FRAMING FOR EVERY NUMBER IN THIS AREA
These systems are opaque, they change without notice, and two runs of the same prompt on the same day can differ. Every measure
above is a description of what you observed in a set you chose, not a measurement of a market. Report the trend and the method
together, never the number alone, and never compare your figures to a published benchmark — no comparable source exists.

ONE SYSTEM, FIVE EMPHASES
Do not build five versions of the Brain. Build one, and use the Client or Segment property to filter it. The differences above are about
sequencing and priority, not about structure — a local service business and a B2B SaaS company need the same Keyword Master, they
just fill different columns of it first.

SECTION 05 · DATA LAYER
The thirty-two databases
Every database in the Brain, specified to the property. Build them in the order given in Section 18 — the
relations depend on it.
Databases 01 to 16 cover strategy, demand, keywords, and content. Databases 17 to 32 cover technical, architecture, authority,
local, AI visibility, and the operating layer. Eight of the thirty-two carry full formula, rollup, and example-record specifications; the
remainder carry properties, relations, views, and workflow, which is everything needed to build them.
THE FICTIONAL REFERENCE COMPANY
Every example record in this document belongs to Northstar Analytics, an invented B2B analytics SaaS company selling to data teams at
mid-market software businesses. Northstar does not exist. Every figure, URL, keyword, and result shown for it is a placeholder written to
show the shape of a completed record, not a claim about anything real.

ROLLUPS
ROLLUP
VIA RELATION
PROPERTY
CALCULATION
WHY IT MATTERS
Cluster keyword count
Cluster
Keyword
Count
Shows whether a cluster has enough query coverage to be
worth building
Cluster average score
Cluster
Priority score
Average
Tells you whether a cluster is commercially worth the build
Page keyword count
Target page
Keyword
Count
Anything above 1 primary keyword per page is a
cannibalization risk
FORMULAS
PRIORITY SCORE
prop("S1 Business relevance") * 3 + prop("S2 Search demand") * 2
+ prop("S3 Intent match") * 3 + prop("S4 Difficulty (inverted)") * 2
+ prop("S5 Conversion potential") * 3 + prop("S6 Current authority") * 1
+ prop("S7 Content gap") * 2 + prop("S8 AI answer potential") * 2
+ prop("S9 Brand fit") * 1 + prop("S10 Strategic value") * 2
Maximum 105. The weights encode a typical B2B situation and are meant to be edited. Record every weight change with a date, or scores stop being comparable across
quarters.
BAND
if(prop("Priority score") >= 80, "Build now",
  if(prop("Priority score") >= 60, "This quarter",
    if(prop("Priority score") >= 40, "When capacity allows",
      if(prop("Priority score") >= 20, "Monitor", "Decline"))))
Bands are thresholds on a judgement-based score. They make the build order consistent between people; they do not make it objectively correct.

AGE (DAYS)
if(empty(prop("Last updated")), toNumber(""),
   dateBetween(now(), prop("Last updated"), "days"))
Age alone does not mean a page needs updating. Use it with Performance — an old page that is still growing should be left alone.

FORMULAS
PRIORITY SCORE
if(prop("Effort") = 0, 0,
   round(prop("Impact") * prop("Pages") * prop("Business")
         * prop("Confidence") / prop("Effort")))
Confidence is the term most teams omit, and the one that prevents a sprint being spent on a hypothesis. Maximum is 625 at effort 1; treat the number as a ranking
device, not a magnitude.
ACTION
if(prop("Confidence") < 3, "Diagnose first", "Fix")

Drafted to Active on the first run; Active to Superseded only when a deliberate rewrite creates a new record
Blocking conditions
Wording cannot be edited on an Active prompt — a change creates a new record at version+1
Definition of done
Wording fixed, type and locale set, good-result definition written, first run recorded
TEMPLATE, CADENCE, ARCHIVE, GATE
Database template
Prompt record with the wording-lock warning and the good-result prompt
Review cadence
Monthly run, quarterly set review
Archive rule
Superseded prompts stay permanently — they anchor the historical trend
Quality gate
Wording never changes on an active prompt; comparability is the entire point

FORMULAS
MENTION RATE
if(prop("Total runs") = 0, "0%",
   round(prop("Mentioned count") / prop("Total runs") * 100) + "%")
Comparable only against your own previous months, at identical prompt wording and the same assistant set. It is not comparable to any published figure or to another
company.
ANSWER COVERAGE
if(prop("Prompts run") = 0, "0%",
   round(prop("Prompts with a mention") / prop("Prompts run") * 100) + "%")
Coverage counts prompts, not runs — a prompt where you appear on one assistant out of three still counts as covered. Report it alongside mention rate, never instead
of it.
SHARE OF AI VOICE
if(prop("All brand mentions") = 0, "0%",
   round(prop("Our mentions") / prop("All brand mentions") * 100) + "%")
A share of the brands named within your own defined prompt set. Changing the prompt set changes the number. It is not market share and must never be described as
such.

Databases 33 and beyond: the libraries
The Prompt Library and SOP Library are databases too. Their schemas are simple and their content is Sections 12 and 13.
DATABASE
PROPERTIES
KEY VIEWS
Prompt Library
Prompt ID · Name · Category · Difficulty · When to use · When not to use ·
Required inputs · Databases used · Prompt body · Expected output · Quality
checklist · Failure conditions · Next prompt · Times used · Last revised
By category · By difficulty · Most used · Never used ·
Recently revised

SOP ID · Name · Purpose · Owner · Trigger · Inputs · Tools · Preconditions ·

Cadence · Related database · Related prompt · Last run · Next due

Overdue
Template Library
Template ID · Name · Operating area · Fields · Completion criterion · Related
SOP · Related prompt · Destination database · Example link
By operating area · By type · Most used
Reports
Period · Metrics with sources and as-of dates · What changed · Hypotheses ·
Three decisions · System changes · Author
Latest · Trend · Decisions outstanding
WHY THE LIBRARIES ARE DATABASES AND NOT PAGES
A prompt on a page gets copied, edited, and forked until three versions exist and nobody knows which is current. A prompt in a database
has one record, a version, a usage count, and a last-revised date. The same argument applies to SOPs and templates — and the Never
Used view on each is how you find out what you built that nobody wanted.
THE DATABASE LAYER

THE RULE THAT APPLIES TO ALL EIGHTY-SEVEN
Every output is a draft. Nothing here goes live, gets sent, or changes a page without a person reviewing it. Where a prompt says it will
name a gap rather than fill it, that behaviour is the point — an answer that invents a benchmark is worse than no answer, because it looks
like research.

PR-26  Stakeholder alignment interview
 STRATEGY · INTERMEDIATE
When to use
Before a quarterly strategy review, to gather what each stakeholder actually needs.
When NOT to use
Do not use it as a substitute for talking to them — it prepares the conversation, it does not replace it.
Required inputs
Stakeholder roles, their functions, the business goal under discussion.
Databases used
Business Goals, SEO Strategy
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are preparing a stakeholder interview for an SEO strategy review.
OBJECTIVE: Surface what each stakeholder needs from search, in their terms.
CONTEXT: Stakeholders: [ROLES AND FUNCTIONS]. Business goal: [GOAL].
Current SEO position: [PASTE].
CONSTRAINTS: One question set per role, six questions maximum each. No leading
questions. Do not assume what any function cares about — the questions exist to
find out.
PROCESS: 1 For each role, identify what they are measured on. 2 Write questions
that surface their actual need rather than their stated request. 3 Include one
question that could reveal a conflict with another function. 4 Note what a
useful answer sounds like.
OUTPUT: A question set per role with the metric each maps to, plus the three
conflicts most likely to surface.
QUALITY CHECKS: No leading questions; every question maps to something the role
is measured on.
MISSING INFORMATION: If a role's metrics are unknown, ask for them first.
HUMAN REVIEW: The SEO lead runs the interviews personally.
Expected output
A question set per role plus the likely conflicts between functions.
Quality checklist
Questions map to what each role is measured on; none are leading.
Failure conditions
Stops if stakeholder metrics are unknown.
Next prompt
PR-27
PR-27  Business goal to SEO metric mapping
 STRATEGY · FOUNDATIONAL
When to use
When a business objective needs translating into something SEO can be held to.
When NOT to use
Do not use it to invent a metric you cannot actually measure with the data you have.
Required inputs
The business objective, available data sources, current baselines.
Databases used
Business Goals, Reports
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are mapping business objectives to measurable SEO outcomes.
OBJECTIVE: Produce a metric chain from a commercial goal to a leading indicator.
CONTEXT: Business objective: [GOAL]. What we can measure today: [DATA SOURCES].
Current baselines with dates: [PASTE].
CONSTRAINTS: Only propose metrics we can actually measure with the listed
sources. Do not propose vanity metrics. Every metric needs a baseline or a plan
to capture one.
PROCESS: 1 Restate the objective. 2 Identify the SEO outcome that serves it.
3 Propose one primary metric and one leading indicator. 4 State where the data
comes from. 5 Flag anything unmeasurable today.
OUTPUT: A metric chain table with source, baseline, and as-of date per metric.
QUALITY CHECKS: Every metric has a named source; unmeasurable items are flagged
rather than substituted.
MISSING INFORMATION: If no baseline exists, produce a baseline capture plan
instead of a metric.
HUMAN REVIEW: The business owner confirms the metric is one they recognize.
Expected output
A metric chain with sources, baselines, and flagged gaps.
Quality checklist
Measurable with listed sources; baselines present or planned.
Failure conditions
Produces a capture plan when no baseline exists.

PR-28  SEO operating charter
 STRATEGY · INTERMEDIATE
When to use
Once, at setup, and revised when the team or the remit changes.
When NOT to use
Do not use it to assign work — it defines decision rights, not tasks.
Required inputs
Team structure, current owners, escalation practice.
Databases used
SEO Strategy, SOP Library
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are writing an SEO operating charter for a team.
OBJECTIVE: Define who decides what, who is consulted, and what escalates.
CONTEXT: Team: [ROLES AND NAMES]. Current pain points in decision-making:
[DESCRIBE]. Escalation practice today: [DESCRIBE].
CONSTRAINTS: Name real roles. Every decision type gets exactly one decider. Do
not create committees. Where a decision genuinely needs two functions, say which
one breaks a tie.
PROCESS: 1 List the recurring decision types. 2 Assign a single decider to each.
3 Identify who must be consulted. 4 Define what escalates, to whom, and within
what time. 5 Flag any decision currently made by nobody.
OUTPUT: A decision-rights table, an escalation route, and the unowned decisions.
QUALITY CHECKS: One decider per decision type; escalation names a person and a
time.
MISSING INFORMATION: Flag decision types where no owner exists rather than
assigning one arbitrarily.
HUMAN REVIEW: The business owner approves decision rights before circulation.
Expected output
A decision-rights table, escalation routes, and unowned decisions named.
Quality checklist
One decider per decision; escalation names a person and a window.
Failure conditions
Flags unowned decisions rather than assigning them.
Next prompt
PR-29
PR-29  Constraint register
 STRATEGY · FOUNDATIONAL
When to use
At the start of any planning cycle, before commitments are made.
When NOT to use
Do not use it to justify inaction — it defines what a realistic plan looks like.
Required inputs
Capacity, budget, dev availability, publishing rate, approval bottlenecks.
Databases used
SEO Strategy
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are documenting the constraints an SEO plan must fit inside.
OBJECTIVE: Make the real limits explicit before the plan is written.
CONTEXT: People and hours: [DESCRIBE]. Publishing capacity: [PIECES/MONTH].
Dev capacity: [DESCRIBE]. Approval steps and their typical duration: [DESCRIBE].
Budget: [DESCRIBE].
CONSTRAINTS: Record actual observed capacity, not stated intent. Where capacity
is unknown, say what to measure for four weeks.
PROCESS: 1 List each constraint with its observed value. 2 Identify which one
binds hardest. 3 State what a plan fitting these constraints looks like in
volume terms. 4 Identify the constraint most cheaply relaxed.
OUTPUT: A constraint register, the binding constraint named, and the realistic
monthly output it implies.
QUALITY CHECKS: Values are observed rather than aspirational; one binding
constraint is named.
MISSING INFORMATION: Propose a four-week measurement rather than estimating.
HUMAN REVIEW: The team confirms the observed capacity is accurate.
Expected output
A constraint register with the binding constraint and realistic output volume.
Quality checklist
Observed values only; one binding constraint identified.
Failure conditions
Proposes measurement rather than estimating unknown capacity.

PR-30  Quarterly strategy review
 STRATEGY · ADVANCED
When to use
Two weeks before each quarter, using the accumulated weekly reports.
When NOT to use
Do not run it without the previous quarter’s reports — the reconstruction is the point.
Required inputs
All weekly reports, bet outcomes, where effort actually went.
Databases used
SEO Strategy, Reports, Decisions & Change Log
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are running a quarterly SEO strategy review.
OBJECTIVE: Compare the stated strategy with what actually happened, and revise.
CONTEXT: Stated strategy: [PASTE]. Weekly reports: [PASTE ALL]. Where effort
went: [DESCRIBE]. Bet outcomes against their signals: [PASTE].
CONSTRAINTS: Reconstruct the strategy from where effort went before comparing.
Name the gap plainly. Do not forecast. Fit the next plan to the constraint
register.
PROCESS: 1 Reconstruct actual strategy from effort. 2 Compare with stated.
3 Assess each bet against its signal — informative or inconclusive. 4 Propose
three bets for next quarter. 5 Write the not-doing list.
OUTPUT: The stated-versus-actual gap, bet verdicts, three new bets with signals
and owners, and the not-doing list.
QUALITY CHECKS: The gap is named explicitly; every new bet has a falsifiable
signal.
MISSING INFORMATION: If reports are missing, say which quarters cannot be
assessed.
HUMAN REVIEW: The business owner approves the revised strategy and versions it.
Expected output
A stated-versus-actual gap, bet verdicts, and three new owned bets.
Quality checklist
The gap is named; every bet signal could be shown false.
Failure conditions
Names unassessable periods rather than inferring them.
Next prompt
PR-31
PR-31  Call mining for demand evidence
 AUDIENCE AND DEMAND · FOUNDATIONAL
When to use
After any batch of sales or support calls with transcripts available.
When NOT to use
Do not use it on summarized notes — paraphrase destroys the language bank.
Required inputs
Verbatim transcripts, labelled by source, role, and date.
Databases used
Customer Problems, Voice of Customer, Question Bank
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are mining call transcripts for demand evidence.
OBJECTIVE: Extract verbatim problems, questions, and objections with sources.
CONTEXT: Transcripts: [PASTE, each labelled with role, date, and call type].
Our ICP: [DESCRIBE].
CONSTRAINTS: Verbatim only — never smooth a quote. Count distinct people, not
mentions. Mark anything raised by one person as a single data point.
PROCESS: 1 Extract every problem statement verbatim with its source. 2 Extract
every question asked, in the form asked. 3 Extract objections separately.
4 Group problems by underlying cause. 5 Note the trigger where stated.
OUTPUT: Three tables — problems with distinct-person counts, questions with
frequency, objections with context — each row carrying its source and date.
QUALITY CHECKS: All quotes verbatim; distinct-person counts, not mention counts.
MISSING INFORMATION: If transcripts are summaries, stop and say the output would
be unreliable.
HUMAN REVIEW: The content lead validates before records enter the database.
Expected output
Problem, question, and objection tables with verbatim quotes and sources.
Quality checklist
Verbatim only; distinct-person counts; single mentions flagged.
Failure conditions
Stops on summarized rather than verbatim input.

PR-32  Review mining
 AUDIENCE AND DEMAND · FOUNDATIONAL
When to use
Quarterly, on category reviews rather than only your own.
When NOT to use
Do not use it as a satisfaction measure for your own product — that is a different job.
Required inputs
Review text with sources and dates, for you and comparable products.
Databases used
Customer Problems, Voice of Customer
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are analysing category review text for demand evidence.
OBJECTIVE: Find what buyers praise and complain about across the category.
CONTEXT: Review text: [PASTE with source and date]. Our category: [DESCRIBE].
CONSTRAINTS: Separate product complaints from onboarding, support, pricing, and
expectation complaints — they need different responses. Rank by frequency, never
by how strongly a review is worded. Cite the source of each pattern.
PROCESS: 1 Categorize every complaint by underlying cause. 2 Count by category.
3 Do the same for praise. 4 Identify which complaints we could credibly address.
5 Extract the exact language buyers use.
OUTPUT: Ranked complaint and praise tables with counts and sources, the
addressable complaints, and the language to reuse.
QUALITY CHECKS: Frequency drives ranking; complaint types separated; single
reviews are not treated as trends.
MISSING INFORMATION: If review dates are absent, note that recency cannot be
assessed.
HUMAN REVIEW: Marketing approves any language before it enters copy.
Expected output
Ranked complaint and praise tables with sources and addressable items.
Quality checklist
Frequency-ranked; categories separated; no single-review trends.
Failure conditions
Flags when recency cannot be assessed.
Next prompt
PR-33
PR-33  Community research
 AUDIENCE AND DEMAND · INTERMEDIATE
When to use
When you need to know how a problem is discussed where you have no presence.
When NOT to use
Do not use it to harvest personal information or to plan covert participation.
Required inputs
Thread content you have gathered, with the community and date noted.
Databases used
Customer Problems, Question Bank, Search Demand
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are analysing community discussion for demand signals.
OBJECTIVE: Understand how the problem is discussed by people who are not our
customers.
CONTEXT: Threads: [PASTE with community and date]. Our category: [DESCRIBE].
CONSTRAINTS: Record no personal information about any individual. Do not propose
covert participation, undisclosed promotion, or astroturfing. Distinguish a
recurring theme from one vocal participant.
PROCESS: 1 Extract the problems described, verbatim. 2 Note what solutions are
recommended and by whom in role terms. 3 Identify vocabulary differences from
our own. 4 Flag recurring unanswered questions. 5 Note where our category is
discussed without us.
OUTPUT: A theme table with frequency, the vocabulary gaps, the unanswered
questions, and where we are absent from the conversation.
QUALITY CHECKS: No personal data recorded; themes distinguished from single
voices; no covert tactics proposed.
MISSING INFORMATION: Note which communities were not covered.
HUMAN REVIEW: Any participation plan is approved by marketing and disclosed.
Expected output
A theme table, vocabulary gaps, unanswered questions, and absence signals.
Quality checklist
No personal data; no covert tactics; themes separated from single voices.
Failure conditions
Names uncovered communities as a limit on the analysis.

PR-34  Jobs-to-be-done mapping
 AUDIENCE AND DEMAND · INTERMEDIATE
When to use
When feature requests and content topics keep drifting apart.
When NOT to use
Do not use it without interview evidence — inventing emotional jobs is worse than having none.
Required inputs
Interview notes, support themes, call summaries with sources.
Databases used
Customer Problems, Topics
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are building a jobs-to-be-done map from evidence.
OBJECTIVE: Identify what customers hire the product to do, and what blocks them.
CONTEXT: Evidence: [PASTE notes, transcripts, tickets with sources]. Our offer:
[DESCRIBE].
CONSTRAINTS: Ground every job in a cited line of evidence. Do not invent
emotional or social jobs nobody expressed. Mark inference clearly.
PROCESS: 1 Identify functional jobs with their evidence. 2 Identify emotional
and social jobs only where expressed. 3 For each, note the trigger situation,
the current alternative, the definition of progress, and the switching anxiety.
4 Rank by evidence weight.
OUTPUT: A jobs table with trigger, alternative, progress definition, anxiety, and
the cited evidence per row.
QUALITY CHECKS: Every job cites a line; unexpressed jobs are omitted rather than
inferred.
MISSING INFORMATION: If only functional evidence exists, say so and omit the
other categories.
HUMAN REVIEW: The content lead validates before it drives topic selection.
Expected output
A jobs table with triggers, alternatives, anxieties, and cited evidence.
Quality checklist
Every job cites evidence; nothing inferred is presented as expressed.
Failure conditions
Omits categories with no evidence rather than inventing them.

PR-35
PR-35  Emerging demand detection
 AUDIENCE AND DEMAND · ADVANCED
When to use
Monthly, to find demand before keyword tools register it.
When NOT to use
Do not use it to justify a topic your ICP has never mentioned.
Required inputs
Recent community, support, and assistant-query evidence with dates.
Databases used
Search Demand, Question Bank, Topics
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are detecting emerging demand ahead of tool data.
OBJECTIVE: Identify questions gaining frequency that tools do not yet show.
CONTEXT: Recent evidence: [PASTE with dates]. Previous cycle's emerging list:
[PASTE]. Our ICP: [DESCRIBE].
CONSTRAINTS: Emerging means rising frequency in recent evidence, not a topic that
sounds novel. Compare against the previous cycle. Do not treat a single new
phrasing as a trend.
PROCESS: 1 Extract questions and phrasings from the last 90 days. 2 Compare with
the previous cycle. 3 Identify what is genuinely new or rising. 4 Check whether
tools show volume — absence supports emergence. 5 Assess ICP relevance.
OUTPUT: An emerging demand table with evidence, cycle-on-cycle change, tool
volume status, and ICP relevance.
QUALITY CHECKS: Comparison against a previous cycle is present; single instances
are not promoted.
MISSING INFORMATION: Without a previous cycle, state that emergence cannot be
assessed and record a baseline instead.
HUMAN REVIEW: The SEO lead decides what enters the keyword list.
Expected output
An emerging demand table with cycle comparison and relevance assessment.
Quality checklist
Cycle comparison present; novelty distinguished from rising frequency.
Failure conditions
Records a baseline rather than claiming emergence on the first run.

PR-36  Pain-to-query mapping
 AUDIENCE AND DEMAND · INTERMEDIATE
When to use
Between demand research and keyword research, to preserve the evidence trail.
When NOT to use
Do not use it to attach a keyword to a pain nobody actually described.
Required inputs
Validated customer problems, the question bank, keyword candidates.
Databases used
Customer Problems, Search Demand, Keyword Master
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are mapping evidenced customer problems to the queries they generate.
OBJECTIVE: Ensure every keyword traces to a stated problem.
CONTEXT: Validated problems: [PASTE with distinct-person counts]. Question bank:
[PASTE]. Keyword candidates: [PASTE].
CONSTRAINTS: A keyword maps to a problem only if the connection is evident from
customer language. Keywords with no traceable problem are flagged, not forced.
PROCESS: 1 For each problem, list the queries someone with it would type or ask.
2 Match against the keyword candidates. 3 Flag keywords with no problem behind
them. 4 Flag problems with no keyword coverage. 5 Note conversational phrasings
separately for the prompt universe.
OUTPUT: A mapping table, the orphan keywords, the uncovered problems, and the
conversational variants.
QUALITY CHECKS: Every mapping is defensible from customer language; orphans are
flagged rather than assigned.
MISSING INFORMATION: If problems are unvalidated, say the mapping is provisional.
HUMAN REVIEW: The SEO specialist confirms before keywords are scored.
Expected output
A mapping table with orphan keywords and uncovered problems named.
Quality checklist
Mappings defensible from customer language; orphans flagged.
Failure conditions
Marks the mapping provisional on unvalidated problems.

PR-37  Search surface map
 AUDIENCE AND DEMAND · INTERMEDIATE
When to use
Quarterly, to know where each question is actually asked.
When NOT to use
Do not use it to justify presence on a surface your buyers do not use.
Required inputs
Question bank, observed buyer behaviour, surface evidence.
Databases used
Search Demand, Question Bank, AI Prompt Universe
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are mapping questions to the surfaces where they are asked.
OBJECTIVE: Decide where coverage is needed, beyond classic search.
CONTEXT: Questions: [PASTE]. What we know about where buyers look: [PASTE
evidence]. Surfaces we can realistically serve: [LIST].
CONSTRAINTS: Base surface assignment on evidence, not assumption. Mark inferred
assignments. Do not recommend a surface we have no capacity to serve.
PROCESS: 1 Assign each question to the surfaces where it is evidenced. 2 Note
which surfaces we currently serve. 3 Identify the highest-frequency questions on
surfaces we ignore. 4 Assess capacity to serve each. 5 Recommend at most two
new surfaces.
OUTPUT: A question-to-surface table, the coverage gaps, and at most two
recommended surfaces with the capacity implication.
QUALITY CHECKS: Assignments cite evidence; recommendations respect capacity.
MISSING INFORMATION: Where surface evidence is absent, mark it unknown rather
than assuming Google.
HUMAN REVIEW: The SEO lead approves any new surface commitment.
Expected output
A question-to-surface map with gaps and capacity-bounded recommendations.
Quality checklist
Evidence-based assignment; at most two new surfaces recommended.
Failure conditions
Marks surfaces unknown rather than defaulting to search.

PR-38  Demand validation pack
 AUDIENCE AND DEMAND · INTERMEDIATE
When to use
Before a topic enters the content build order.
When NOT to use
Do not use it to retro-justify a topic already commissioned.
Required inputs
The candidate topic, all supporting evidence, existing coverage.
Databases used
Customer Problems, Search Demand, Topics, Content
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are validating whether a topic has real demand behind it.
OBJECTIVE: Produce a pass or fail verdict against six validation criteria.
CONTEXT: Candidate topic: [DESCRIBE]. Evidence: [PASTE all]. Existing coverage:
[PASTE]. Our differentiating evidence: [DESCRIBE].
CONSTRAINTS: Six criteria — three or more distinct people, a commercial path,
something specific to say, customer vocabulary, a known surface, and an
extractable answer. All six must pass, or the verdict is fail.
PROCESS: 1 Test each criterion against the evidence. 2 State pass or fail per
criterion with the evidence. 3 Give an overall verdict. 4 If it fails, say what
evidence would change it.
OUTPUT: A six-criterion verdict table, an overall verdict, and the evidence
needed to change a fail.
QUALITY CHECKS: All six tested; the overall verdict follows the criteria rather
than enthusiasm.
MISSING INFORMATION: A criterion with no evidence is a fail, not a pass by
default.
HUMAN REVIEW: The content lead may override, but must record why.
Expected output
A six-criterion verdict with the evidence needed to reverse a fail.
Quality checklist
All six criteria tested; missing evidence fails rather than passes.
Failure conditions
Treats absent evidence as failure, not neutrality.

PR-39  Seed keyword expansion
 KEYWORDS · FOUNDATIONAL
When to use
At the start of keyword research, after demand evidence exists.
When NOT to use
Do not use it as the starting point — seeds should come from customer language.
Required inputs
Seed terms drawn from the demand layer, our offer, competitor vocabulary.
Databases used
Search Demand, Keyword Master
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are expanding seed keywords into a candidate set.
OBJECTIVE: Produce a broad candidate list organized by the sixteen query types.
CONTEXT: Seeds from customer language: [PASTE]. Our offer: [DESCRIBE].
Competitor vocabulary: [PASTE]. Markets: [LIST].
CONSTRAINTS: Expand from customer vocabulary first, internal naming last. Do not
invent search volumes. Organize output by query type so gaps are visible.
PROCESS: 1 Expand each seed across the sixteen types. 2 Mark which came from
customer language and which from our own. 3 Identify types with no candidates —
those are coverage gaps. 4 Flag terms only we use.
OUTPUT: A candidate list grouped by query type, source-labelled, with the empty
types named as gaps.
QUALITY CHECKS: Customer-language origin is labelled; no invented volumes; empty
types surfaced.
MISSING INFORMATION: If no customer vocabulary was supplied, say the expansion
will reflect internal language only.
HUMAN REVIEW: The SEO specialist prunes before scoring.
Expected output
A type-organized candidate list with source labels and coverage gaps.
Quality checklist
Customer language prioritized and labelled; no invented volumes.
Failure conditions
Warns when expansion reflects internal vocabulary only.

PR-40  BOFU query mining
 KEYWORDS · INTERMEDIATE
When to use
When the pipeline is full of traffic content and short of revenue-adjacent pages.
When NOT to use
Do not use it on a site with no commercial pages to point BOFU traffic at.
Required inputs
Product knowledge, pricing structure, competitor names, sales objections.
Databases used
Keyword Master, Content, Question Bank
COPY-PASTE PROMPT
ROLE: You are mining bottom-of-funnel queries.
OBJECTIVE: Find the queries closest to a purchase decision that we do not cover.
CONTEXT: What we sell: [DESCRIBE]. Pricing structure: [DESCRIBE]. Competitors:
[LIST]. Sales objections: [PASTE]. Existing commercial pages: [LIST].
CONSTRAINTS: BOFU means the searcher is choosing, not learning. Do not include
informational queries. Every suggestion must have a plausible commercial
destination on our site or be flagged as needing one.
PROCESS: 1 Generate pricing, comparison, alternative, review, and integration
queries. 2 Generate objection-shaped queries from the sales objections.
3 Map each to an existing page or flag it as a gap. 4 Rank by proximity to
decision.
OUTPUT: A BOFU query table with intent, destination or gap, and decision
proximity ranking.
QUALITY CHECKS: No informational queries included; every query has a destination
or an explicit gap.
MISSING INFORMATION: If pricing structure is undisclosed, note which query types
cannot be served honestly.
HUMAN REVIEW: The SEO lead confirms before commissioning commercial pages.
Expected output
A BOFU query table ranked by decision proximity with destinations or gaps.
Quality checklist
Only decision-stage queries; every query has a destination or a flagged gap.
Failure conditions
Notes which queries cannot be served without disclosed pricing.
